========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, doctor. Thank you for seeing me. I have to admit, sitting in this chair is a bit surreal for me. As a healthcare social worker, I'm usually the one on the other side of things, coordinating care and helping families, which I've done ever since I finished grad school. So I usually pride myself on keeping everything together, you know? I'm the one people come to for help. 

But... well, I think what finally made me bite the bullet and come in today—which honestly took me a few weeks to actually do after finding your clinic's number—is that my appetite has just been completely all over the place for months now. It's so frustrating and just very unlike me. I’ll have these long stretches where I end up completely skipping dinner, and I've actually been losing weight without even trying to. My work slacks are getting noticeably loose, which my sister actually pointed out to me just the other day when she stopped by. And then, on other nights, instead of actually making a proper meal, I'll just stand in the kitchen and pick at saltine crackers or grab handfuls of dry cereal, just because the entire process of cooking for myself feels so utterly pointless. I just look at the stove and think, why bother dirtying a pan and a plate just for me? It just feels like too much effort for no reason, and since this weird relationship with food has been going on for months now, I finally realized I couldn't just keep brushing it off and pretending it was normal.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, actually. I think that's part of what's been so unsettling for me lately. I mean, I still try to keep up with my routine, because I’ve always believed in the importance of structure, especially with my clients. So I still go through the motions of things I used to genuinely love. Like my garden, for instance. I have these raised beds in the backyard, and I used to spend hours out there on the weekends, pruning and weeding, just really taking pride in it. Now, I still go out there to water them because I feel guilty if I just let them die, but... I don't know, it just feels so flat. There's no joy in it anymore. I just stand there with the hose and wait for the dirt to look wet, and then I go back inside. 

And it's the same with my book club. I’ve been going to this same group for, gosh, maybe five years now? We meet the second Tuesday of every month, and I used to be the one baking something to bring or coming with all these highlighted passages to discuss. But lately... well, I still show up, because I don't want to be rude or have them worry about me, but most of the time I just sit there feeling completely disconnected from the conversation. The last two times, I actually made up an excuse about having an early morning meeting the next day just so I could leave early. And there are some weekends where I think about starting a new project or going to the farmers market, and I just... don't. I can't even bring myself to start. It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything, and I’m just walking through a black-and-white movie of my own life, trying to pretend I still care about the plot.

Well, yes, actually. I mean, it’s not just the past two weeks, it’s been going on for a while now, but definitely these last couple of weeks have just felt incredibly heavy. It’s hard to even describe, really. It’s like this pervasive sort of gray cloud that just settles over me from the moment I wake up. I’ll open my eyes and look at the ceiling, and instead of thinking about what I need to do that day, I just feel this overwhelming sense of sadness that I can’t quite pinpoint. And it just stays with me, you know? 

I’ll be at work, sitting in a care conference with a family, trying to help them figure out a discharge plan for their father, and I’m nodding and saying all the right things, but underneath it all, I just feel so incredibly down. It’s like there’s this constant hum of misery in the background of everything I do. And it doesn’t really lift as the day goes on, either. By the time I get home, I’m just so emotionally exhausted from carrying it around all day that I usually just end up sitting on the couch in silence. I used to at least put on some soft jazz or a podcast while I decompressed, but lately, even that feels like too much noise. So, yes, to answer your question, it’s been pretty much every day, all day, just this constant, heavy feeling that I can’t seem to shake, no matter how much I try to rationalize it or push through it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, absolutely. It’s actually been one of the hardest things to manage, to be perfectly honest with you. I mean, I try to get my full eight hours of sleep—I’ve always been very strict about my sleep hygiene, keeping the bedroom cool, no screens an hour before bed, all those things I recommend to my own patients—but lately, it just doesn't seem to make a single bit of difference. 

Even on days when my caseload is not unusually heavy... like, say, a quiet Wednesday where I only have a couple of straightforward discharge placements and maybe one routine family update, nothing like the intense crisis interventions I sometimes have to navigate... I still feel this profound, overwhelming exhaustion. It quite literally feels like I am dragging my physical body through the hospital corridors. You know those long, endless stretches of linoleum in the wards? Walking down them lately feels like I’m moving underwater, or like my legs are made of lead. 

And then, by the time I finally clock out and make the drive home, which is really only about a twenty-minute commute on a good day, I am just utterly depleted. I walk through my front door, put my keys in the bowl, and I just have absolutely nothing left in the tank. I mentioned earlier about the food and not wanting to cook, and a huge part of that is just being way too tired to even fathom chopping vegetables or standing over a stove. But it’s not just the cooking. I used to have this whole evening routine where I’d do some light yoga, or at the very least, I'd take ten or fifteen minutes to stretch out my lower back after sitting in those terrible, unergonomic hospital chairs all day. I haven't done that in months. I am literally too tired to stretch. 

I just kind of collapse onto the furniture. And my phone will buzz with personal messages—my sister checking in, or a friend from grad school sending a link to an article, or someone asking about my weekend—and I’ll look at the screen, and the mere thought of typing out a response, even just a quick "I'm good, you?", just feels like I'm being asked to climb a mountain. I just leave them unread for days because I physically and mentally do not have the energy to formulate an answer. It’s just... it's exhausting being this exhausted, if that makes any sense.

I mean, it’s not just the past two weeks, it’s been going on for a while now, but definitely these last couple of weeks have just felt incredibly heavy. It’s hard to even describe, really. It’s like this pervasive sort of gray cloud that just settles over me from the moment I wake up. I’ll open my eyes and look at the ceiling, and instead of thinking about what I need to do that day, I just feel this overwhelming sense of sadness that I can’t quite pinpoint. And it just stays with me, you know? \n\nI’ll be at work, sitting in a care conference with a family, trying to help them figure out a discharge plan for their father, and I’m nodding and saying all the right things, but underneath it all, I just feel so incredibly down. It’s like there’s this constant hum of misery in the background of everything I do. And it doesn’t really lift as the day goes on, either. By the time I get home, I’m just so emotionally exhausted from carrying it around all day that I usually just end up sitting on the couch in silence. I used to at least put on some soft jazz or a podcast while I decompressed, but lately, even that feels like too much noise. So, yes, to answer your question, it’s been pretty much every day, all day, just this constant, heavy feeling that I can’t seem to shake, no matter how much I try to rationalize it or push through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or noticed a lack of energy, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. It’s actually been one of the hardest things to manage, to be perfectly honest with you. I mean, I try to get my full eight hours of sleep—I’ve always been very strict about my sleep hygiene, keeping the bedroom cool, no screens an hour before bed, all those things I recommend to my own patients—but lately, it just doesn't seem to make a single bit of difference. \n\nEven on days when my caseload is not unusually heavy... like, say, a quiet Wednesday where I only have a couple of straightforward discharge placements and maybe one routine family update, nothing like the intense crisis interventions I sometimes have to navigate... I still feel this profound, overwhelming exhaustion. It quite literally feels like I am dragging my physical body through the hospital corridors. You know those long, endless stretches of linoleum in the wards? Walking down them lately feels like I’m moving underwater, or like my legs are made of lead. \n\nAnd then, by the time I finally clock out and make the drive home, which is really only about a twenty-minute commute on a good day, I am just utterly depleted. I walk through my front door, put my keys in the bowl, and I just have absolutely nothing left in the tank. I mentioned earlier about the food and not wanting to cook, and a huge part of that is just being way too tired to even fathom chopping vegetables or standing over a stove. But it’s not just the cooking. I used to have this whole evening routine where I’d do some light yoga, or at the very least, I'd take ten or fifteen minutes to stretch out my lower back after sitting in those terrible, unergonomic hospital chairs all day. I haven't done that in months. I am literally too tired to stretch. \n\nI just kind of collapse onto the furniture. And my phone will buzz with personal messages—my sister checking in, or a friend from grad school sending a link to an article, or someone asking about my weekend—and I’ll look at the screen, and the mere thought of typing out a response, even just a quick \"I'm good, you?\", just feels like I'm being asked to climb a mountain. I just leave them unread for days because I physically and mentally do not have the energy to formulate an answer. It’s just... it's exhausting being this exhausted, if that makes any sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not really. I mean, I haven't noticed anything like that. I know what you mean, though—I see it all the time with some of the patients on the ward, where they're pacing the halls or wringing their hands, or, on the flip side, they're just sitting there like statues, taking ten seconds to answer a simple question. But for me? No, I don't think so. 

I mean, I'm exhausted, like I was just saying, so maybe I'm not exactly sprinting down the hallways at work the way I might have a few years ago when I was trying to track down a doctor before they went off shift. But I wouldn't say I'm moving in slow motion or anything like that. I still get from point A to point B at a relatively normal pace, even if it feels incredibly difficult to do so. And I certainly haven't felt restless or fidgety. If anything, the idea of pacing or bouncing my leg just sounds like it requires energy that I simply don't have right now. 

When I'm alone, like when I'm sitting on the couch after work, I'm pretty still. I just sit there. Sometimes I'll realize I've been staring at the same spot on the wall for twenty minutes without moving a muscle, but it's not because I'm physically incapable of moving, it's just that I don't have the desire or the energy to do anything else. And as for speaking slowly... well, I suppose you can be the judge of that, since we've been talking for a bit now. I don't feel like my speech is any different. I still have to articulate complex discharge plans and coordinate with various departments all day, and no one has mentioned that I'm talking unusually slow or anything. So, no, I don't think I've experienced either of those things, really. Just the profound tiredness and the heavy feeling, mostly.

It just feels like too much effort for no reason, and since this weird relationship with food has been going on for months now, I finally realized I couldn't just keep brushing it off and pretending it was normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I think that's part of what's been so unsettling for me lately. I mean, I still try to keep up with my routine, because I’ve always believed in the importance of structure, especially with my clients. So I still go through the motions of things I used to genuinely love. Like my garden, for instance. I have these raised beds in the backyard, and I used to spend hours out there on the weekends, pruning and weeding, just really taking pride in it. Now, I still go out there to water them because I feel guilty if I just let them die, but... I don't know, it just feels so flat. There's no joy in it anymore. I just stand there with the hose and wait for the dirt to look wet, and then I go back inside. \n\nAnd it's the same with my book club. I’ve been going to this same group for, gosh, maybe five years now? We meet the second Tuesday of every month, and I used to be the one baking something to bring or coming with all these highlighted passages to discuss. But lately... well, I still show up, because I don't want to be rude or have them worry about me, but most of the time I just sit there feeling completely disconnected from the conversation. The last two times, I actually made up an excuse about having an early morning meeting the next day just so I could leave early. And there are some weekends where I think about starting a new project or going to the farmers market, and I just... don't. I can't even bring myself to start. It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything, and I’m just walking through a black-and-white movie of my own life, trying to pretend I still care about the plot.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I mean, it’s not just the past two weeks, it’s been going on for a while now, but definitely these last couple of weeks have just felt incredibly heavy. It’s hard to even describe, really. It’s like this pervasive sort of gray cloud that just settles over me from the moment I wake up. I’ll open my eyes and look at the ceiling, and instead of thinking about what I need to do that day, I just feel this overwhelming sense of sadness that I can’t quite pinpoint. And it just stays with me, you know? \n\nI’ll be at work, sitting in a care conference with a family, trying to help them figure out a discharge plan for their father, and I’m nodding and saying all the right things, but underneath it all, I just feel so incredibly down. It’s like there’s this constant hum of misery in the background of everything I do. And it doesn’t really lift as the day goes on, either. By the time I get home, I’m just so emotionally exhausted from carrying it around all day that I usually just end up sitting on the couch in silence. I used to at least put on some soft jazz or a podcast while I decompressed, but lately, even that feels like too much noise. So, yes, to answer your question, it’s been pretty much every day, all day, just this constant, heavy feeling that I can’t seem to shake, no matter how much I try to rationalize it or push through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or noticed a lack of energy, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. It’s actually been one of the hardest things to manage, to be perfectly honest with you. I still feel this profound, overwhelming exhaustion. It quite literally feels like I am dragging my physical body through the hospital corridors. Walking down them lately feels like I’m moving underwater, or like my legs are made of lead. \n\nAnd then, by the time I finally clock out and make the drive home, which is really only about a twenty-minute commute on a good day, I am just utterly depleted. I walk through my front door, put my keys in the bowl, and I just have absolutely nothing left in the tank. I mentioned earlier about the food and not wanting to cook, and a huge part of that is just being way too tired to even fathom chopping vegetables or standing over a stove. I just leave them unread for days because I physically and mentally do not have the energy to formulate an answer. And my phone will buzz with personal messages—my sister checking in, or a friend from grad school sending a link to an article, or someone asking about my weekend—and I’ll look at the screen, and the mere thought of typing out a response, even just a quick \"I'm good, you?\", just feels like I'm being asked to climb a mountain. I just leave them unread for days because I physically and mentally do not have the energy to formulate an answer. It’s just... it's exhausting being this exhausted, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, feeling restless or fidgety, especially when you're alone?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I haven't noticed anything like that. I know what you mean, though—I see it all the time with some of the patients on the ward, where they're pacing the halls or wringing their hands, or, on the flip side, they're just sitting there like statues, taking ten seconds to answer a simple question. But for me? No, I don't think so. \n\nI mean, I'm exhausted, like I was just saying, so maybe I'm not exactly sprinting down the hallways at work the way I might have a few years ago when I was trying to track down a doctor before they went off shift. But I wouldn't say I'm moving in slow motion or anything like that. I still get from point A to point B at a relatively normal pace, even if it feels incredibly difficult to do so. And I certainly haven't felt restless or fidgety. If anything, the idea of pacing or bouncing my leg just sounds like it requires energy that I simply don't have right now. \n\nWhen I'm alone, like when I'm sitting on the couch after work, I'm pretty still. I just sit there. Sometimes I'll realize I've been staring at the same spot on the wall for twenty minutes without moving a muscle, but it's not because I'm physically incapable of moving, it's just that I don't have the desire or the energy to do anything else. And as for speaking slowly... well, I suppose you can be the judge of that, since we've been talking for a bit now. I don't feel like my speech is any different. I still have to articulate complex discharge plans and coordinate with various departments all day, and no one has mentioned that I'm talking unusually slow or anything. So, no, I don't think I've experienced either of those things, really. Just the profound tiredness and the heavy feeling, mostly.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling like you’re not good enough, or been overly critical of yourself, maybe even feeling guilty about things that you wouldn’t normally feel guilty about?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, absolutely. I mean, it’s funny you ask that, because it’s almost like a constant, endless loop playing in my head lately. I've always been a bit of a perfectionist—you kind of have to be in my line of work, making sure every detail of a patient's care plan is completely buttoned up, you know? But lately, that critical eye has just been turned entirely inward, and it’s just exhausting. 

Mostly, it’s about my marriage. I just... I keep replaying the marriage over and over in my mind, analyzing every little conversation, every argument, every quiet evening where I maybe should have said something and didn't. And I just keep telling myself that I must have failed at something incredibly basic. Like, I’m a social worker, for heaven's sake, I have a master's degree in understanding human behavior and family systems, so how could I not figure out my own relationship? I sit there on the couch in the evenings, like I mentioned before, and instead of just resting, my brain is just picking apart everything I did wrong. I tell myself I wasn't attentive enough, or I brought too much stress home from the hospital, or I just fundamentally lacked whatever it is that makes a person a good partner. 

And the logical part of my brain—the part that counsels families going through crises every single day—knows that’s not entirely fair. I mean, part of me knows that relationships are so much more complicated than that, that it takes two people, and it’s never just one person’s complete and utter failure. I would never let one of my clients take 100 percent of the blame for a dynamic that involved two people. But that logical, professional voice is just so quiet right now compared to the guilt. The guilt is just so incredibly loud. 

I feel guilty for things I said five years ago, guilty for not trying harder, guilty for... I don't know, just not being enough. It’s like I’m putting myself on trial every single evening, and I’m the judge and the jury, and the verdict is always that I’m the one who ruined everything. I just can't seem to shut it off, even when I know it's not entirely rational to take all of that on myself. It just sits there in my chest, making me feel like an absolute fraud.

It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything, and I’m just walking through a black-and-white movie of my own life, trying to pretend I still care about the plot.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I mean, it’s not just the past two weeks, it’s been going on for a while now, but definitely these last couple of weeks have just felt incredibly heavy. It’s hard to even describe, really. It’s like this pervasive sort of gray cloud that just settles over me from the moment I wake up. I’ll open my eyes and look at the ceiling, and instead of thinking about what I need to do that day, I just feel this overwhelming sense of sadness that I can’t quite pinpoint. And it just stays with me, you know? \n\nI’ll be at work, sitting in a care conference with a family, trying to help them figure out a discharge plan for their father, and I’m nodding and saying all the right things, but underneath it all, I just feel so incredibly down. It’s like there’s this constant hum of misery in the background of everything I do. And it doesn’t really lift as the day goes on, either. By the time I get home, I’m just so emotionally exhausted from carrying it around all day that I usually just end up sitting on the couch in silence. I used to at least put on some soft jazz or a podcast while I decompressed, but lately, even that feels like too much noise. So, yes, to answer your question, it’s been pretty much every day, all day, just this constant, heavy feeling that I can’t seem to shake, no matter how much I try to rationalize it or push through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or noticed a lack of energy, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. It’s actually been one of the hardest things to manage, to be perfectly honest with you. I mean, I try to get my full eight hours of sleep—I’ve always been very strict about my sleep hygiene, keeping the bedroom cool, no screens an hour before bed, all those things I recommend to my own patients—but lately, it just doesn't seem to make a single bit of difference. \n\nEven on days when my caseload is not unusually heavy... like, say, a quiet Wednesday where I only have a couple of straightforward discharge placements and maybe one routine family update, nothing like the intense crisis interventions I sometimes have to navigate... I still feel this profound, overwhelming exhaustion. It quite literally feels like I am dragging my physical body through the hospital corridors. You know those long, endless stretches of linoleum in the wards? Walking down them lately feels like I’m moving underwater, or like my legs are made of lead. \n\nAnd then, by the time I finally clock out and make the drive home, which is really only about a twenty-minute commute on a good day, I am just utterly depleted. I walk through my front door, put my keys in the bowl, and I just have absolutely nothing left in the tank. I mentioned earlier about the food and not wanting to cook, and a huge part of that is just being way too tired to even fathom chopping vegetables or standing over a stove. But it’s not just the cooking. I used to have this whole evening routine where I’d do some light yoga, or at the very least, I'd take ten or fifteen minutes to stretch out my lower back after sitting in those terrible, unergonomic hospital chairs all day. I haven't done that in months. I am literally too tired to stretch. \n\nI just kind of collapse onto the furniture. And my phone will buzz with personal messages—my sister checking in, or a friend from grad school sending a link to an article, or someone asking about my weekend—and I’ll look at the screen, and the mere thought of typing out a response, even just a quick \"I'm good, you?\", just feels like I'm being asked to climb a mountain. I just leave them unread for days because I physically and mentally do not have the energy to formulate an answer. It’s just... it's exhausting being this exhausted, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, feeling restless or fidgety, especially when you're alone?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I haven't noticed anything like that. I know what you mean, though—I see it all the time with some of the patients on the ward, where they're pacing the halls or wringing their hands, or, on the flip side, they're just sitting there like statues, taking ten seconds to answer a simple question. But for me? No, I don't think so. \n\nI mean, I'm exhausted, like I was just saying, so maybe I'm not exactly sprinting down the hallways at work the way I might have a few years ago when I was trying to track down a doctor before they went off shift. But I wouldn't say I'm moving in slow motion or anything like that. I still get from point A to point B at a relatively normal pace, even if it feels incredibly difficult to do so. And I certainly haven't felt restless or fidgety. If anything, the idea of pacing or bouncing my leg just sounds like it requires energy that I simply don't have right now. \n\nWhen I'm alone, like when I'm sitting on the couch after work, I'm pretty still. I just sit there. Sometimes I'll realize I've been staring at the same spot on the wall for twenty minutes without moving a muscle, but it's not because I'm physically incapable of moving, it's just that I don't have the desire or the energy to do anything else. And as for speaking slowly... well, I suppose you can be the judge of that, since we've been talking for a bit now. I don't feel like my speech is any different. I still have to articulate complex discharge plans and coordinate with various departments all day, and no one has mentioned that I'm talking unusually slow or anything. So, no, I don't think I've experienced either of those things, really. Just the profound tiredness and the heavy feeling, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re not good enough, or been overly critical of yourself, maybe even feeling guilty about things that you wouldn’t normally feel guilty about?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I mean, it’s funny you ask that, because it’s almost like a constant, endless loop playing in my head lately. I've always been a bit of a perfectionist—you kind of have to be in my line of work, making sure every detail of a patient's care plan is completely buttoned up, you know? But lately, that critical eye has just been turned entirely inward, and it’s just exhausting. \n\nMostly, it’s about my marriage. I just... I keep replaying the marriage over and over in my mind, analyzing every little conversation, every argument, every quiet evening where I maybe should have said something and didn't. And I just keep telling myself that I must have failed at something incredibly basic. Like, I’m a social worker, for heaven's sake, I have a master's degree in understanding human behavior and family systems, so how could I not figure out my own relationship? I sit there on the couch in the evenings, like I mentioned before, and instead of just resting, my brain is just picking apart everything I did wrong. I tell myself I wasn't attentive enough, or I brought too much stress home from the hospital, or I just fundamentally lacked whatever it is that makes a person a good partner. \n\nAnd the logical part of my brain—the part that counsels families going through crises every single day—knows that’s not entirely fair. I mean, part of me knows that relationships are so much more complicated than that, that it takes two people, and it’s never just one person’s complete and utter failure. I would never let one of my clients take 100 percent of the blame for a dynamic that involved two people. But that logical, professional voice is just so quiet right now compared to the guilt. The guilt is just so incredibly loud. \n\nI feel guilty for things I said five years ago, guilty for not trying harder, guilty for... I don't know, just not being enough. It’s like I’m putting myself on trial every single evening, and I’m the judge and the jury, and the verdict is always that I’m the one who ruined everything. I just can't seem to shut it off, even when I know it's not entirely rational to take all of that on myself. It just sits there in my chest, making me feel like an absolute fraud.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s actually the waking up that’s the problem. I mean, getting to sleep isn't usually the issue. Like I mentioned earlier, I try to be so disciplined about my evening routine. I do the whole wind-down process, I make sure I’m in bed at a perfectly reasonable hour—usually by ten, sometimes ten-thirty if I got caught up reading a case file—and I usually drift off okay because, frankly, I'm just so physically depleted by the end of the day. 

But then... it’s like my brain has this cruel internal clock. Almost every single night, right around four in the morning, my eyes just snap open. And it’s not a gentle waking up, either. It’s this immediate, jarring rush of thoughts. The second I'm awake, my mind just starts racing at a hundred miles an hour. I’ll start agonizing over the bills, wondering if I need to adjust my budget now that I'm on a single income, or I'll suddenly panic about my work notes. I'll lie there trying to remember if I properly documented a complex discharge plan for a patient I saw three days ago, or if I remembered to follow up with a specific community agency. 

And of course, if it’s not the bills or the hospital, it’s the divorce. My mind just goes right back to analyzing the marriage, just like I was telling you before, picking apart every detail of the separation while the rest of the world is pitch black and completely silent. And the worst part is, I am still so incredibly tired. My body feels like it weighs a thousand pounds, and my eyes are burning, but I just cannot get back to sleep once my brain starts doing that. So I just lie there. I lie there in the dark, completely exhausted, just staring at the ceiling and watching the minutes tick by on the clock until my alarm finally goes off at six. And by the time it actually rings, I feel like I've already lived an entire stressful day before I've even put my feet on the floor.

As a healthcare social worker, I'm usually the one on the other side of things, coordinating care and helping families, which I've done ever since I finished grad school. So I usually pride myself on keeping everything together, you know? I'm the one people come to for help. \n\nBut... well, I think what finally made me bite the bullet and come in today—which honestly took me a few weeks to actually do after finding your clinic's number—is that my appetite has just been completely all over the place for months now. It just feels like too much effort for no reason, and since this weird relationship with food has been going on for months now, I finally realized I couldn't just keep brushing it off and pretending it was normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I think that's part of what's been so unsettling for me lately. I mean, I still try to keep up with my routine, because I’ve always believed in the importance of structure, especially with my clients. Now, I still go out there to water them because I feel guilty if I just let them die, but... I don't know, it just feels so flat. There's no joy in it anymore. I just stand there with the hose and wait for the dirt to look wet, and then I go back inside. \n\nAnd it's the same with my book club. I’ve been going to this same group for, gosh, maybe five years now? We meet the second Tuesday of every month, and I used to be the one baking something to bring or coming with all these highlighted passages to discuss. But lately... well, I still show up, because I don't want to be rude or have them worry about me, but most of the time I just sit there feeling completely disconnected from the conversation. The last two times, I actually made up an excuse about having an early morning meeting the next day just so I could leave early. And there are some weekends where I think about starting a new project or going to the farmers market, and I just... don't. I can't even bring myself to start. It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything, and I’m just walking through a black-and-white movie of my own life, trying to pretend I still care about the plot.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I mean, it’s not just the past two weeks, it’s been going on for a while now, but definitely these last couple of weeks have just felt incredibly heavy. It’s hard to even describe, really. It’s like this pervasive sort of gray cloud that just settles over me from the moment I wake up. I’ll open my eyes and look at the ceiling, and instead of thinking about what I need to do that day, I just feel this overwhelming sense of sadness that I can’t quite pinpoint. And it just stays with me, you know? \n\nI’ll be at work, sitting in a care conference with a family, trying to help them figure out a discharge plan for their father, and I’m nodding and saying all the right things, but underneath it all, I just feel so incredibly down. It’s like there’s this constant hum of misery in the background of everything I do. And it doesn’t really lift as the day goes on, either. By the time I get home, I’m just so emotionally exhausted from carrying it around all day that I usually just end up sitting on the couch in silence. I used to at least put on some soft jazz or a podcast while I decompressed, but lately, even that feels like too much noise. So, yes, to answer your question, it’s been pretty much every day, all day, just this constant, heavy feeling that I can’t seem to shake, no matter how much I try to rationalize it or push through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or noticed a lack of energy, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. It’s actually been one of the hardest things to manage, to be perfectly honest with you. I mean, I try to get my full eight hours of sleep—I’ve always been very strict about my sleep hygiene, keeping the bedroom cool, no screens an hour before bed, all those things I recommend to my own patients—but lately, it just doesn't seem to make a single bit of difference. \n\nEven on days when my caseload is not unusually heavy... like, say, a quiet Wednesday where I only have a couple of straightforward discharge placements and maybe one routine family update, nothing like the intense crisis interventions I sometimes have to navigate... I still feel this profound, overwhelming exhaustion. It quite literally feels like I am dragging my physical body through the hospital corridors. You know those long, endless stretches of linoleum in the wards? Walking down them lately feels like I’m moving underwater, or like my legs are made of lead. \n\nAnd then, by the time I finally clock out and make the drive home, which is really only about a twenty-minute commute on a good day, I am just utterly depleted. I walk through my front door, put my keys in the bowl, and I just have absolutely nothing left in the tank. I mentioned earlier about the food and not wanting to cook, and a huge part of that is just being way too tired to even fathom chopping vegetables or standing over a stove. But it’s not just the cooking. I used to have this whole evening routine where I’d do some light yoga, or at the very least, I'd take ten or fifteen minutes to stretch out my lower back after sitting in those terrible, unergonomic hospital chairs all day. I haven't done that in months. I am literally too tired to stretch. \n\nI just kind of collapse onto the furniture. And my phone will buzz with personal messages—my sister checking in, or a friend from grad school sending a link to an article, or someone asking about my weekend—and I’ll look at the screen, and the mere thought of typing out a response, even just a quick \"I'm good, you?\", just feels like I'm being asked to climb a mountain. I just leave them unread for days because I physically and mentally do not have the energy to formulate an answer. It’s just... it's exhausting being this exhausted, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, feeling restless or fidgety, especially when you're alone?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I haven't noticed anything like that. I know what you mean, though—I see it all the time with some of the patients on the ward, where they're pacing the halls or wringing their hands, or, on the flip side, they're just sitting there like statues, taking ten seconds to answer a simple question. But for me? No, I don't think so. \n\nI mean, I'm exhausted, like I was just saying, so maybe I'm not exactly sprinting down the hallways at work the way I might have a few years ago when I was trying to track down a doctor before they went off shift. But I wouldn't say I'm moving in slow motion or anything like that. I still get from point A to point B at a relatively normal pace, even if it feels incredibly difficult to do so. And I certainly haven't felt restless or fidgety. If anything, the idea of pacing or bouncing my leg just sounds like it requires energy that I simply don't have right now. \n\nWhen I'm alone, like when I'm sitting on the couch after work, I'm pretty still. I just sit there. Sometimes I'll realize I've been staring at the same spot on the wall for twenty minutes without moving a muscle, but it's not because I'm physically incapable of moving, it's just that I don't have the desire or the energy to do anything else. And as for speaking slowly... well, I suppose you can be the judge of that, since we've been talking for a bit now. I don't feel like my speech is any different. I still have to articulate complex discharge plans and coordinate with various departments all day, and no one has mentioned that I'm talking unusually slow or anything. So, no, I don't think I've experienced either of those things, really. Just the profound tiredness and the heavy feeling, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re not good enough, or been overly critical of yourself, maybe even feeling guilty about things that you wouldn’t normally feel guilty about?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I mean, it’s funny you ask that, because it’s almost like a constant, endless loop playing in my head lately. I've always been a bit of a perfectionist—you kind of have to be in my line of work, making sure every detail of a patient's care plan is completely buttoned up, you know? But lately, that critical eye has just been turned entirely inward, and it’s just exhausting. \n\nMostly, it’s about my marriage. I just... I keep replaying the marriage over and over in my mind, analyzing every little conversation, every argument, every quiet evening where I maybe should have said something and didn't. And I just keep telling myself that I must have failed at something incredibly basic. Like, I’m a social worker, for heaven's sake, I have a master's degree in understanding human behavior and family systems, so how could I not figure out my own relationship? I sit there on the couch in the evenings, like I mentioned before, and instead of just resting, my brain is just picking apart everything I did wrong. I tell myself I wasn't attentive enough, or I brought too much stress home from the hospital, or I just fundamentally lacked whatever it is that makes a person a good partner. \n\nAnd the logical part of my brain—the part that counsels families going through crises every single day—knows that’s not entirely fair. I mean, part of me knows that relationships are so much more complicated than that, that it takes two people, and it’s never just one person’s complete and utter failure. I would never let one of my clients take 100 percent of the blame for a dynamic that involved two people. But that logical, professional voice is just so quiet right now compared to the guilt. The guilt is just so incredibly loud. \n\nI feel guilty for things I said five years ago, guilty for not trying harder, guilty for... I don't know, just not being enough. It’s like I’m putting myself on trial every single evening, and I’m the judge and the jury, and the verdict is always that I’m the one who ruined everything. I just can't seem to shut it off, even when I know it's not entirely rational to take all of that on myself. It just sits there in my chest, making me feel like an absolute fraud.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping, like not being able to fall asleep or waking up in the middle of the night, or on the flip side, have you found yourself sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: It’s actually the waking up that’s the problem. Almost every single night, right around four in the morning, my eyes just snap open. And it’s not a gentle waking up, either. It’s this immediate, jarring rush of thoughts. No, I don't think so. \n\nI mean, I'm exhausted, like I was just saying, so maybe I'm not exactly sprinting down the hallways at work the way I might have a few years ago when I was trying to track down a doctor before they went off shift. And I certainly haven't felt restless or fidgety. If anything, the idea of pacing or bouncing my leg just sounds like it requires energy that I simply don't have right now. \n\nWhen I'm alone, like when I'm sitting on the couch after work, I'm pretty still. I just sit there. Sometimes I'll realize I've been staring at the same spot on the wall for twenty minutes without moving a muscle, but it's not because I'm physically incapable of moving, it's just that I don't have the desire or the energy to do anything else. I still have to articulate complex discharge plans and coordinate with various departments all day, and no one has mentioned that I'm talking unusually slow or anything. So, no, I don't think I've experienced either of those things, really. Just the profound tiredness and the heavy feeling, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re not good enough, or been overly critical of yourself, maybe even feeling guilty about things that you wouldn’t normally feel guilty about?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I mean, it’s funny you ask that, because it’s almost like a constant, endless loop playing in my head lately. I've always been a bit of a perfectionist—you kind of have to be in my line of work, making sure every detail of a patient's care plan is completely buttoned up, you know? But lately, that critical eye has just been turned entirely inward, and it’s just exhausting. \n\nMostly, it’s about my marriage. I just... I tell myself I wasn't attentive enough, or I brought too much stress home from the hospital, or I just fundamentally lacked whatever it is that makes a person a good partner. \n\nAnd the logical part of my brain—the part that counsels families going through crises every single day—knows that’s not entirely fair. I mean, part of me knows that relationships are so much more complicated than that, that it takes two people, and it’s never just one person’s complete and utter failure. I just can't seem to shut it off, even when I know it's not entirely rational to take all of that on myself. Almost every single night, right around four in the morning, my eyes just snap open. And it’s not a gentle waking up, either. It’s this immediate, jarring rush of thoughts. I’ll start agonizing over the bills, wondering if I need to adjust my budget now that I'm on a single income, or I'll suddenly panic about my work notes. I'll lie there trying to remember if I properly documented a complex discharge plan for a patient I saw three days ago, or if I remembered to follow up with a specific community agency. \n\nAnd of course, if it’s not the bills or the hospital, it’s the divorce. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose that’s a very accurate way to put it. It's just... flat. Everything just feels incredibly flat lately. 

I’ve always been someone who loved being busy, having hobbies outside of the hospital to decompress. Like my garden, for instance. I have these beautiful hydrangeas and a whole vegetable patch out back that I’ve tended to for, gosh, maybe fifteen years now. And it used to be my absolute sanctuary. I’d come home from a long day of dealing with intense family crises on the ward, and I’d just go out there and water the plants, pull weeds, get my hands in the dirt. It was always so grounding for me. But lately? I mean, I still go through the motions. I’ll walk out there with the hose or the watering can because I know I *should*, because I don't want the plants to die after all these years, but I just stand there and... I don't feel anything. There's no joy in it whatsoever. Half the time I just look at the weeds and I can't even bring myself to start pulling them, so I just turn the water off and go right back inside. 

And it’s not just the solitary things, either. It’s social things, too. I’ve been part of this book club with some women from the neighborhood for almost a decade. We read a good mix of things—historical fiction, biographies—and it was always the highlight of my month. The wine, the conversation, just getting away from everything and having a laugh. I still try to show up. I really do try. I’ll get dressed, I'll drive over there, I'll sit on someone's couch with a glass of Pinot... but I'm just not really *there*. The conversation is happening all around me, and I’m just totally disconnected from it. It all just feels so dull. The last three times I went, I ended up making some excuse about having an early shift at the hospital the next day and leaving early. 

It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything I used to love. I try to force myself, because my social worker brain says, "Keep doing your activities, maintain your routine, behavioral activation is important," but it’s just so hard when there's no reward, no pleasure in it at all. It just becomes another chore on a list of things I don't have the energy for. So, yes, the lack of interest has definitely seeped into pretty much everything.

I’m Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thank you for seeing me. I have to admit, sitting in this chair is a bit surreal for me. As a healthcare social worker, I'm usually the one on the other side of things, coordinating care and helping families, which I've done ever since I finished grad school. So I usually pride myself on keeping everything together, you know? I'm the one people come to for help. \n\nBut... well, I think what finally made me bite the bullet and come in today—which honestly took me a few weeks to actually do after finding your clinic's number—is that my appetite has just been completely all over the place for months now. It's so frustrating and just very unlike me. I’ll have these long stretches where I end up completely skipping dinner, and I've actually been losing weight without even trying to. My work slacks are getting noticeably loose, which my sister actually pointed out to me just the other day when she stopped by. And then, on other nights, instead of actually making a proper meal, I'll just stand in the kitchen and pick at saltine crackers or grab handfuls of dry cereal, just because the entire process of cooking for myself feels so utterly pointless. I just look at the stove and think, why bother dirtying a pan and a plate just for me? It just feels like too much effort for no reason, and since this weird relationship with food has been going on for months now, I finally realized I couldn't just keep brushing it off and pretending it was normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I think that's part of what's been so unsettling for me lately. I mean, I still try to keep up with my routine, because I’ve always believed in the importance of structure, especially with my clients. So I still go through the motions of things I used to genuinely love. Like my garden, for instance. I have these raised beds in the backyard, and I used to spend hours out there on the weekends, pruning and weeding, just really taking pride in it. Now, I still go out there to water them because I feel guilty if I just let them die, but... I don't know, it just feels so flat. There's no joy in it anymore. I just stand there with the hose and wait for the dirt to look wet, and then I go back inside. \n\nAnd it's the same with my book club. I’ve been going to this same group for, gosh, maybe five years now? We meet the second Tuesday of every month, and I used to be the one baking something to bring or coming with all these highlighted passages to discuss. But lately... well, I still show up, because I don't want to be rude or have them worry about me, but most of the time I just sit there feeling completely disconnected from the conversation. The last two times, I actually made up an excuse about having an early morning meeting the next day just so I could leave early. And there are some weekends where I think about starting a new project or going to the farmers market, and I just... don't. I can't even bring myself to start. It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything, and I’m just walking through a black-and-white movie of my own life, trying to pretend I still care about the plot.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I mean, it’s not just the past two weeks, it’s been going on for a while now, but definitely these last couple of weeks have just felt incredibly heavy. It’s hard to even describe, really. It’s like this pervasive sort of gray cloud that just settles over me from the moment I wake up. I’ll open my eyes and look at the ceiling, and instead of thinking about what I need to do that day, I just feel this overwhelming sense of sadness that I can’t quite pinpoint. And it just stays with me, you know? \n\nI’ll be at work, sitting in a care conference with a family, trying to help them figure out a discharge plan for their father, and I’m nodding and saying all the right things, but underneath it all, I just feel so incredibly down. It’s like there’s this constant hum of misery in the background of everything I do. And it doesn’t really lift as the day goes on, either. By the time I get home, I’m just so emotionally exhausted from carrying it around all day that I usually just end up sitting on the couch in silence. I used to at least put on some soft jazz or a podcast while I decompressed, but lately, even that feels like too much noise. So, yes, to answer your question, it’s been pretty much every day, all day, just this constant, heavy feeling that I can’t seem to shake, no matter how much I try to rationalize it or push through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or noticed a lack of energy, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. It’s actually been one of the hardest things to manage, to be perfectly honest with you. I mean, I try to get my full eight hours of sleep—I’ve always been very strict about my sleep hygiene, keeping the bedroom cool, no screens an hour before bed, all those things I recommend to my own patients—but lately, it just doesn't seem to make a single bit of difference. \n\nEven on days when my caseload is not unusually heavy... like, say, a quiet Wednesday where I only have a couple of straightforward discharge placements and maybe one routine family update, nothing like the intense crisis interventions I sometimes have to navigate... I still feel this profound, overwhelming exhaustion. It quite literally feels like I am dragging my physical body through the hospital corridors. You know those long, endless stretches of linoleum in the wards? Walking down them lately feels like I’m moving underwater, or like my legs are made of lead. \n\nAnd then, by the time I finally clock out and make the drive home, which is really only about a twenty-minute commute on a good day, I am just utterly depleted. I walk through my front door, put my keys in the bowl, and I just have absolutely nothing left in the tank. I mentioned earlier about the food and not wanting to cook, and a huge part of that is just being way too tired to even fathom chopping vegetables or standing over a stove. But it’s not just the cooking. I used to have this whole evening routine where I’d do some light yoga, or at the very least, I'd take ten or fifteen minutes to stretch out my lower back after sitting in those terrible, unergonomic hospital chairs all day. I haven't done that in months. I am literally too tired to stretch. \n\nI just kind of collapse onto the furniture. And my phone will buzz with personal messages—my sister checking in, or a friend from grad school sending a link to an article, or someone asking about my weekend—and I’ll look at the screen, and the mere thought of typing out a response, even just a quick \"I'm good, you?\", just feels like I'm being asked to climb a mountain. I just leave them unread for days because I physically and mentally do not have the energy to formulate an answer. It’s just... it's exhausting being this exhausted, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, feeling restless or fidgety, especially when you're alone?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I haven't noticed anything like that. I know what you mean, though—I see it all the time with some of the patients on the ward, where they're pacing the halls or wringing their hands, or, on the flip side, they're just sitting there like statues, taking ten seconds to answer a simple question. But for me? No, I don't think so. \n\nI mean, I'm exhausted, like I was just saying, so maybe I'm not exactly sprinting down the hallways at work the way I might have a few years ago when I was trying to track down a doctor before they went off shift. But I wouldn't say I'm moving in slow motion or anything like that. I still get from point A to point B at a relatively normal pace, even if it feels incredibly difficult to do so. And I certainly haven't felt restless or fidgety. If anything, the idea of pacing or bouncing my leg just sounds like it requires energy that I simply don't have right now. \n\nWhen I'm alone, like when I'm sitting on the couch after work, I'm pretty still. I just sit there. Sometimes I'll realize I've been staring at the same spot on the wall for twenty minutes without moving a muscle, but it's not because I'm physically incapable of moving, it's just that I don't have the desire or the energy to do anything else. And as for speaking slowly... well, I suppose you can be the judge of that, since we've been talking for a bit now. I don't feel like my speech is any different. I still have to articulate complex discharge plans and coordinate with various departments all day, and no one has mentioned that I'm talking unusually slow or anything. So, no, I don't think I've experienced either of those things, really. Just the profound tiredness and the heavy feeling, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re not good enough, or been overly critical of yourself, maybe even feeling guilty about things that you wouldn’t normally feel guilty about?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I mean, it’s funny you ask that, because it’s almost like a constant, endless loop playing in my head lately. I've always been a bit of a perfectionist—you kind of have to be in my line of work, making sure every detail of a patient's care plan is completely buttoned up, you know? But lately, that critical eye has just been turned entirely inward, and it’s just exhausting. \n\nMostly, it’s about my marriage. I just... I keep replaying the marriage over and over in my mind, analyzing every little conversation, every argument, every quiet evening where I maybe should have said something and didn't. And I just keep telling myself that I must have failed at something incredibly basic. Like, I’m a social worker, for heaven's sake, I have a master's degree in understanding human behavior and family systems, so how could I not figure out my own relationship? I sit there on the couch in the evenings, like I mentioned before, and instead of just resting, my brain is just picking apart everything I did wrong. I tell myself I wasn't attentive enough, or I brought too much stress home from the hospital, or I just fundamentally lacked whatever it is that makes a person a good partner. \n\nAnd the logical part of my brain—the part that counsels families going through crises every single day—knows that’s not entirely fair. I mean, part of me knows that relationships are so much more complicated than that, that it takes two people, and it’s never just one person’s complete and utter failure. I would never let one of my clients take 100 percent of the blame for a dynamic that involved two people. But that logical, professional voice is just so quiet right now compared to the guilt. The guilt is just so incredibly loud. \n\nI feel guilty for things I said five years ago, guilty for not trying harder, guilty for... I don't know, just not being enough. It’s like I’m putting myself on trial every single evening, and I’m the judge and the jury, and the verdict is always that I’m the one who ruined everything. I just can't seem to shut it off, even when I know it's not entirely rational to take all of that on myself. It just sits there in my chest, making me feel like an absolute fraud.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping, like not being able to fall asleep or waking up in the middle of the night, or on the flip side, have you found yourself sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: It’s actually the waking up that’s the problem. I mean, getting to sleep isn't usually the issue. Like I mentioned earlier, I try to be so disciplined about my evening routine. I do the whole wind-down process, I make sure I’m in bed at a perfectly reasonable hour—usually by ten, sometimes ten-thirty if I got caught up reading a case file—and I usually drift off okay because, frankly, I'm just so physically depleted by the end of the day. \n\nBut then... it’s like my brain has this cruel internal clock. Almost every single night, right around four in the morning, my eyes just snap open. And it’s not a gentle waking up, either. It’s this immediate, jarring rush of thoughts. The second I'm awake, my mind just starts racing at a hundred miles an hour. I’ll start agonizing over the bills, wondering if I need to adjust my budget now that I'm on a single income, or I'll suddenly panic about my work notes. I'll lie there trying to remember if I properly documented a complex discharge plan for a patient I saw three days ago, or if I remembered to follow up with a specific community agency. \n\nAnd of course, if it’s not the bills or the hospital, it’s the divorce. My mind just goes right back to analyzing the marriage, just like I was telling you before, picking apart every detail of the separation while the rest of the world is pitch black and completely silent. And the worst part is, I am still so incredibly tired. My body feels like it weighs a thousand pounds, and my eyes are burning, but I just cannot get back to sleep once my brain starts doing that. So I just lie there. I lie there in the dark, completely exhausted, just staring at the ceiling and watching the minutes tick by on the clock until my alarm finally goes off at six. And by the time it actually rings, I feel like I've already lived an entire stressful day before I've even put my feet on the floor.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in most activities, or has this lack of interest extended to nearly everything you do?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose that’s a very accurate way to put it. It's just... flat. Everything just feels incredibly flat lately. \n\nI’ve always been someone who loved being busy, having hobbies outside of the hospital to decompress. Like my garden, for instance. I have these beautiful hydrangeas and a whole vegetable patch out back that I’ve tended to for, gosh, maybe fifteen years now. And it used to be my absolute sanctuary. I’d come home from a long day of dealing with intense family crises on the ward, and I’d just go out there and water the plants, pull weeds, get my hands in the dirt. It was always so grounding for me. But lately? I mean, I still go through the motions. I’ll walk out there with the hose or the watering can because I know I *should*, because I don't want the plants to die after all these years, but I just stand there and... I don't feel anything. There's no joy in it whatsoever. Half the time I just look at the weeds and I can't even bring myself to start pulling them, so I just turn the water off and go right back inside. \n\nAnd it’s not just the solitary things, either. It’s social things, too. I’ve been part of this book club with some women from the neighborhood for almost a decade. We read a good mix of things—historical fiction, biographies—and it was always the highlight of my month. The wine, the conversation, just getting away from everything and having a laugh. I still try to show up. I really do try. I’ll get dressed, I'll drive over there, I'll sit on someone's couch with a glass of Pinot... but I'm just not really *there*. The conversation is happening all around me, and I’m just totally disconnected from it. It all just feels so dull. The last three times I went, I ended up making some excuse about having an early shift at the hospital the next day and leaving early. \n\nIt’s like the color has just been drained out of everything I used to love. I try to force myself, because my social worker brain says, \"Keep doing your activities, maintain your routine, behavioral activation is important,\" but it’s just so hard when there's no reward, no pleasure in it at all. It just becomes another chore on a list of things I don't have the energy for. So, yes, the lack of interest has definitely seeped into pretty much everything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Almost every single night, right around four in the morning, my eyes just snap open. And it’s not a gentle waking up, either. It’s this immediate, jarring rush of thoughts. The second I'm awake, my mind just starts racing at a hundred miles an hour. I’ll start agonizing over the bills, wondering if I need to adjust my budget now that I'm on a single income, or I'll suddenly panic about my work notes. I'll lie there trying to remember if I properly documented a complex discharge plan for a patient I saw three days ago, or if I remembered to follow up with a specific community agency. \n\nAnd of course, if it’s not the bills or the hospital, it’s the divorce. My mind just goes right back to analyzing the marriage, just like I was telling you before, picking apart every detail of the separation while the rest of the world is pitch black and completely silent. And the worst part is, I am still so incredibly tired. My body feels like it weighs a thousand pounds, and my eyes are burning, but I just cannot get back to sleep once my brain starts doing that. So I just lie there. I lie there in the dark, completely exhausted, just staring at the ceiling and watching the minutes tick by on the clock until my alarm finally goes off at six. And by the time it actually rings, I feel like I've already lived an entire stressful day before I've even put my feet on the floor.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in most activities, or has this lack of interest extended to nearly everything you do?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose that’s a very accurate way to put it. It's just... flat. Everything just feels incredibly flat lately. \n\nI’ve always been someone who loved being busy, having hobbies outside of the hospital to decompress. Like my garden, for instance. I have these beautiful hydrangeas and a whole vegetable patch out back that I’ve tended to for, gosh, maybe fifteen years now. And it used to be my absolute sanctuary. I’d come home from a long day of dealing with intense family crises on the ward, and I’d just go out there and water the plants, pull weeds, get my hands in the dirt. It was always so grounding for me. But lately? I mean, I still go through the motions. I’ll walk out there with the hose or the watering can because I know I *should*, because I don't want the plants to die after all these years, but I just stand there and... I don't feel anything. There's no joy in it whatsoever. Half the time I just look at the weeds and I can't even bring myself to start pulling them, so I just turn the water off and go right back inside. \n\nAnd it’s not just the solitary things, either. It’s social things, too. I’ve been part of this book club with some women from the neighborhood for almost a decade. We read a good mix of things—historical fiction, biographies—and it was always the highlight of my month. The wine, the conversation, just getting away from everything and having a laugh. I still try to show up. I really do try. I’ll get dressed, I'll drive over there, I'll sit on someone's couch with a glass of Pinot... but I'm just not really *there*. The conversation is happening all around me, and I’m just totally disconnected from it. It all just feels so dull. The last three times I went, I ended up making some excuse about having an early shift at the hospital the next day and leaving early. \n\nIt’s like the color has just been drained out of everything I used to love. I try to force myself, because my social worker brain says, \"Keep doing your activities, maintain your routine, behavioral activation is important,\" but it’s just so hard when there's no reward, no pleasure in it at all. It just becomes another chore on a list of things I don't have the energy for. So, yes, the lack of interest has definitely seeped into pretty much everything.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced episodes like this before, where you felt down, lost interest in things, or had trouble functioning, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’ll walk out there with the hose or the watering can because I know I *should*, because I don't want the plants to die after all these years, but I just stand there and... I don't feel anything. There's no joy in it whatsoever. Half the time I just look at the weeds and I can't even bring myself to start pulling them, so I just turn the water off and go right back inside. \n\nAnd it’s not just the solitary things, either. It’s social things, too. I’ve been part of this book club with some women from the neighborhood for almost a decade. We read a good mix of things—historical fiction, biographies—and it was always the highlight of my month. The wine, the conversation, just getting away from everything and having a laugh. I still try to show up. I really do try. I’ll get dressed, I'll drive over there, I'll sit on someone's couch with a glass of Pinot... but I'm just not really *there*. The conversation is happening all around me, and I’m just totally disconnected from it. It all just feels so dull. The last three times I went, I ended up making some excuse about having an early shift at the hospital the next day and leaving early. \n\nIt’s like the color has just been drained out of everything I used to love. I try to force myself, because my social worker brain says, \"Keep doing your activities, maintain your routine, behavioral activation is important,\" but it’s just so hard when there's no reward, no pleasure in it at all. It just becomes another chore on a list of things I don't have the energy for. So, yes, the lack of interest has definitely seeped into pretty much everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced episodes like this before, where you felt down, lost interest in things, or had trouble functioning, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: No... no, I really haven't. And I think that’s part of why I’m struggling so much to wrap my head around it, because this is entirely new territory for me. I mean, sure, I’ve been stressed before. Everyone gets stressed. When I was going through my master’s program, working full-time and doing my practicum hours, I was exhausted. And, you know, working in a hospital setting for as long as I have, you see a lot of tragedy, you carry a lot of heavy emotional weight from the patients, and there are days where you just come home and want to cry. \n\nBut even then, even on the absolute hardest days on the ward, I always bounced back. I’d have a good night’s sleep, or I'd spend a Saturday out in the dirt with my plants, and I’d feel restored. I always had my baseline of... well, of just being me. Capable, energetic, engaged. I could always find my footing again.\n\nBut this? This is just completely different. I've never had this... this total absence of feeling, or this inability to just shake off the guilt and the exhaustion. It’s like I’ve crossed a line into something I just don't recognize at all. I keep looking back, you know, using my clinical brain to try and analyze my twenties or my thirties, wondering if maybe I had a milder version of this and just powered through it because I was younger and had more stamina. But honestly, no. I've always been the rock. I’m the one my friends call when they’re falling apart, I’m the one who coordinates the complex care plans, I’m the one who holds everything together for everyone else. \n\nTo be the one who is suddenly just... unraveling, and not even caring about the book club or the hydrangeas? It’s completely foreign. So, yes, to answer your question, this is absolutely the very first time I’ve ever experienced anything even remotely like this. And I have to admit, it's really frightening, because none of my usual coping mechanisms are working, and I just don't know how to navigate it when I've never been in this place before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like I was just saying a moment ago... no, not even a milder version. I know why you're asking again, I really do. In my line of work, we always look for that history, right? It wasn't this... this pervasive, heavy blanket where I couldn't function or didn't care about my garden or my friends. I still found comfort in things back then. I still wanted to be around people who loved me. 

So, no, not even a less severe version. I've never had a period of time where I just couldn't find joy in my life or struggled to get through my basic daily functions. This whole experience, this total lack of interest and this absolute flatness... it’s entirely unprecedented for me. It's just so strange to be sitting on this side of the clipboard, you know? To be answering these questions and realizing that I have absolutely no prior frame of reference for what my own brain is putting me through right now. I've always been the resilient one, so this is just... it's all brand new, and honestly, it's very humbling.
